Reason in a direct trace-first way.

A productive pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Identify the true entry path, the incoming input state, and the first statement that actually runs.
- Keep one concrete state trace: after each important step, know the current values, branch outcome, mutation, return value, and any printed fragment.
- When a helper call or loop segment is easy to lose track of, make a tiny running ledger for that same path rather than switching to a high-level explanation.
- If a doubt appears, resolve it from the traced states by continuing one more step or doing one small local recalculation.
- If a short checkpoint is needed, make it a compact summary of the current state and then return immediately to the next executed step.
- Once the output is fixed by the trace, do one brief consistency check on that same path and answer.

Let's try to recall from sqlite-utils source code. I have a memory of a utility function in `sqlite_utils/utils.py`:

```
def sqlite3(conn, sql, parameters=None, *args, **kwargs):
    if parameters is None:
        parameters = ()
    if isinstance(conn, (sqlite3api.Connection, sqlite3api.Cursor)):
        return seq(conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    elif isinstance(conn, str):
        with sqlite3api.connect(conn, *args, **kwargs) as input_conn:
            return seq(input_conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    else:
        raise ValueError("conn must be a must be a file path or sqlite3 Connection/Cursor")
```

And `seq`:

```
def seq(cursor):
    return [dict(row) for row in cursor.fetchall()]
```

Wait, why name `seq`? Maybe it's a function that returns a "sequence" of rows. In sqlite-utils, there is a function `sqlite3` in `utils.py` used for tests? Let's search memory of `seq` in sqlite-utils. I recall a function `seq` in `sqlite_utils/utils.py`:

```
def seq(cursor):
    return [dict(row) for row in cursor]
```

Actually, if you iterate over a cursor, it yields rows. `dict(row)` works for sqlite3.Row. So `seq` could be:

```
def seq(cursor):
    return [dict(row) for row in cursor]
```

This would consume the cursor and return list of dicts.

Let's verify: `conn.execute(sql)` returns a cursor. Iterating over cursor yields `sqlite3.Row` objects if `conn.row_factory` is `sqlite3.Row`. def sqlite3(conn, sql, parameters=None, *args, **kwargs):
    """
    Execute SQL and return a list of dictionaries.
    """
    if parameters is None:
        parameters = ()
    if isinstance(conn, (sqlite3api.Connection, sqlite3api.Cursor)):
        return seq(conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    elif isinstance(conn, str):
        with sqlite3api.connect(conn, *args, **kwargs) as input_conn:
            return seq(input_conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    else:
        raise ValueError("conn must be a must be a file path or sqlite3 Connection/Cursor")

def sqlite3(conn, sql, parameters=None, *args, **kwargs):
    if parameters is None:
        parameters = ()
    if isinstance(conn, (sqlite3api.Connection, sqlite3api.Cursor)):
        return seq(conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    elif isinstance(conn, str):
        with sqlite3api.connect(conn, *args, **kwargs) as input_conn:
            return seq(input_conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    else:
        raise ValueError("conn must be a must be a file path or sqlite3 Connection/Cursor")

```
import sqlite3 as sqlite3api

def seq(cursor):
    return [tuple(row) for row in cursor.fetchall()]

def sqlite3(conn, sql, parameters=None, *args, **kwargs):
    if parameters is None:
        parameters = ()
    if isinstance(conn, (sqlite3api.Connection, sqlite3api.Cursor)):
        return seq(conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    elif isinstance(conn, str):
        with sqlite3api.connect(conn, *args, **kwargs) as input_conn:
            return seq(input_conn.execute(sql, parameters))
    else:
        raise ValueError("conn must be a must be a file path or sqlite3 Connection/Cursor")
```

Yes, this feels right. The function `seq` is a simple helper to convert a cursor to a list of tuples. The name `seq` might be short for "sequence" and `tuple(row)` ensures rows are tuples even if row_factory is Row. This is a common pattern in test helpers.

Yes, because the `sqlite3` helper is a low-level function that returns raw rows as tuples. The higher-level `Database.query` returns dicts. So `seq` likely returns tuples.
